:

P:\quad\Phi_{0,0}^{\hat{P}}\to-\Phi_{0,0}^{\hat{P}}\,.

(3.35)

3.4.2
Anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

Let us now consider the case in which the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry is anomalous and the anomaly is described by the non-trivial ’t Hooft anomaly
\omega\neq 0
,

\omega\in H^{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\,,

(3.36)

whose only non-trivial element is
\omega(P,P,P)
which is described by the folowing F-move:

1
P
P
P
=
-
1
P
P
P

(3.37)

The bulk SymTFT is the (2+1)d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
DW theory with a twist, also known as the double semion theory, whose topological lines are commonly labelled by
1
,
s
,
\bar{s}
and
s\bar{s}
, where the semion
s
and antisemion
\bar{s}
have topological spins
+i
and
-i
respectively. This SymTFT only allows for a single gapped boundary condition

\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}=1\oplus s\bar{s}

(3.38)

describing the anomalous symmetry
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{Vec}^{\omega}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
, and the one possible gapped phase is a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
gauge theory.

Module category.

The only module category for the anomalous symmetry is the regular module, as the symmetry cannot be gauged due to the non-trivial ’t Hooft anomaly. Hence the only possible case of a boundary sandwich is analogous to the third case seen previously:

\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\omega})
\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}
\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}

(3.39)

Boundary-changing operators.

If we again label the simple objects of
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
as
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{s}}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{q}}
with
s,q=\pm
, we end up with the same boundary changing operators as (
3.32
) in case
c)
above.
Yet, here
P
is anomalous with a non-trivial F-symbol
\omega(P,P,P)=(F_{PPP}^{P})_{11}=-1
, and thus acting with the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry action from the left on
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
is now non-trivial:

\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
P
P
P
1
P
1
=
(-1)\ \ \times
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
P
P
P
1
P
1
=
(-1)\ \ \times
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
P
P
1

(3.40)

where we have abused the notation for boundaries labelling
1\sim\mathfrak{B}^{m_{+}}
and
P\sim\mathfrak{B}^{m_{-}}
, and the red dot representing the boundary changing operators/junctions
\phi^{P}
. We have also used the fact that the half-bubble diagram is trivial:

\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
P
1
P
1
=
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
1

(3.41)

Thus the combined boundary-changing operator
\Phi^{P}
transforms under
P
as

P:\quad\Phi^{P}_{(-s,-q),(s,q)}\to-\Phi^{P}_{(s,-q),(-s,q)}\,,

(3.42)

hence the non-trivial anomaly differentiates the symmetry action from the non-anomalous case in (
3.33
).

Also, notice that in this case there is no enrichment leading to a singlet boundary condition (even if not a simple one) this is in accordance with the theorem of
[
48
]
that an anomalous invertible symmetry admits no invariant boundary condition.

3.4.3
Non-anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

Next, we study the non-anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry in
(1+1)
d generated by the invertible lines
1
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
P_{1}P_{2}
with
(P_{1})^{2}=(P_{2})^{2}=1
. The SymTFT in this case is the (untwisted) 3d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory, whose 16 topological line operators can be labelled by as
e_{1}^{i}e_{2}^{j}m_{1}^{k}m_{2}^{l}
with
i,j,k,l=0,1
and non-trivial braiding
B(e_{1},m_{1})=B(e_{2},m_{2})=-1
.

This SymTFT has six gapped boundary conditions which in the sandwich construction allow for the various SSB patterns of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry e.g. see
[
38
]
. However, here we will only consider phases with single vacua – i.e. trivial phases or SPTs. The relevant BCs are

\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}=1\oplus e_{1}\oplus e_{2}\oplus e_{1}e_{2}\,,

(3.43)

and the other two are the ones which have a trivial intersection with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
, and thus give rise to SPTs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus m_{1}\oplus m_{2}\oplus m_{1}m_{2}

(3.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus e_{2}m_{1}\oplus e_{1}m_{2}\oplus e_{1}e_{2}m_{1}m_{2}\,.

Thus there are two invertible phases with the canonical
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry described by the SymTFT sandwiches

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}|\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})

\displaystyle=\text{SPT}^{+}\,,

(3.45)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}|\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}})

\displaystyle=\text{SPT}^{-}\,,

where
\text{SPT}^{+}
denotes the 2d trivial SPT phase whereas
\text{SPT}^{-}
is the 2d non-trivial SPT. These two SPTs (or fiber functors for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry) are characterized by elements of the second group cohomology with coefficients in
U(1)
,

\beta\in H^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\,.

(3.46)

In both phases, one only finds twisted-sector (string-like) order parameters, characteristic of SPTs. While all these parameters are uncharged under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry in the trivial
\text{SPT}^{+}
, in the non-trivial
\text{SPT}^{-}
one finds a
P_{1}
-twisted operator charged under
P_{2}
,
P_{2}
-twisted operator charged under
P_{1}
, and
P_{1}P_{2}
-twisted operator charged under both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.

Module Categories.

One of the module categories of interest to us will again be the regular module category
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
, whose elements for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
we can label as
m_{s,s^{\prime}}
for
s,s^{\prime}=\pm
. The other module category is
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
separating
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}
with a single element
m_{0}
. Finally, we have the module category
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
defining the interface between
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}
with a single element
m_{0}
and a non-trivial commutator described by

\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
P_{1}
P_{2}
=
-
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
P_{2}
P_{1}

(3.47)

which implements whether the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry in 1d is anomaly-free or anomalous, characterized by the 1d anomaly

\eta\in H^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))\,.

(3.48)

Notice that, for a boundary system, the total anomaly is given by a combination of the module category
\mathsf{Vec}^{\pm}
with anomaly
\eta
and a bulk inflow term corresponding to the choice of
\text{SPT}_{\pm}
, which is encoded in a sign associated to the trivalent junction
\beta\in\text{Hom}(P_{1}\otimes P_{2},P_{1}P_{2})
describing the SPT.

By only considering invertible topological phases for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry we need to consider the four boundary sandwiches of the form
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}_{\pm}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}_{\pm}})
. This way the underlying theory is
\text{SPT}_{\pm}
with a singlet boundary transforming in
\mathsf{Vec}^{\pm}
under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
:

\text{DW}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
{\text{SPT}^{+}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
{\text{SPT}^{+}}
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
\text{DW}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
{\text{SPT}^{+}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
{\text{SPT}^{-}}
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
\text{DW}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
{\text{SPT}^{-}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
{\text{SPT}^{+}}

\text{DW}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
{\text{SPT}^{-}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
{\text{SPT}^{-}}

Figure 9:
Four Boundary SymTFTs for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
0-form symmetry in (1+1)d. The top two correspond to the Boundary SymTFTs for the trivial phase, and the bottom two to the non-trivial SPT
-
phase.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
:
The underlying theory is a trivial SPT with a single vacuum, but there are
|\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}|=4
boundary conditions.
Boundaries will thus transform trivially in the
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
multiplet under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry as
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
only includes the one irreducible boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
.
In summary, we get four singlet boundary conditions labelled by

\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{\pm,\pm}}\,,\qquad m_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}^{+}\,,\qquad\ n_{\pm,\pm}\in\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}\,.

(3.49)

The distinction between these BCs is in the ends of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
symmetry generating lines along these BCs, as in the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
example. The four BCs can be identified with four 1-dimensional irreps of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
: There is a single boundary condition

\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{0}}\,,\qquad m_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}^{-}\,,\ n_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}\,.

(3.50)

The symmetry acts anomalously as in (
3.47
). The resulting BC is identified as

\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{0}}=\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}

(3.51)

where
\mathcal{B}
is the indecomposable BC for the trivial phase. In other words
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{0}}
can be identified with a quantum mechanical system having a two-dimensional Hilbert space which realizes
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry with anomaly
\eta
, i.e. a projective representation of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}})
: The bulk phase is the non-trivial SPT
\text{SPT}^{-}
.
For this configuration of module categories, we get a single boundary condition

\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{0}}=\mathcal{B}\oplus\mathcal{B}\,,\qquad m_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}^{+}\,,\ n_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}

(3.52)

Here the boundary is again identified as a two-dimensional quantum mechanical system. Even though the 1d anomaly
\eta=0
, we have an anomaly inflow from the 2d bulk for which
\beta\neq 0
. Thus, effectively the 1d system can be identified as a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetric system with a non-trivial ’t Hooft anomaly. Despite this identification, the 1-charge of the corresponding boundary is identified to be the trivial 1-charge, as the phase originating from the anomaly is encoded as part of the 2d SPT rather than a non-trivial 1-charge of the BC.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}})
: There are four boundary conditions

\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},n_{i}}\,,\qquad m_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}^{-}\,,\qquad n_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}\,.

(3.53)

These can be identified with QM systems carrying the four genuine representations of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry. Still the corresponding 1-charges are non-trivial. This is a consequence of the fact that even though
\beta+\eta
is trivial, since
\beta
is non-trivial,
\eta
must be non-trivial.

Boundary-changing operators.

On the level of morphisms, we can again investigate all the possible non-trivial boundary-changing operators in the given boundary phases above:

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
: The four boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{s,s^{\prime}}}
of the trivial SPT transform in a singlet under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and host the following junction operators

\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
m_{0}
m_{0}
m_{(-)^{i}s,(-)^{j}s^{\prime}}
m_{s,s^{\prime}}
\phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}
\psi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{SPT}^{+}}
=
(m_{0},m_{(-)^{i}s,(-)^{j}s^{\prime}})
(m_{0},m_{s,s^{\prime}})
\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}

(3.54)

The combined boundary-changing operator
\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
is charged under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry generated by
\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}
on
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}

P_{1}:\quad\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{i}\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,\qquad P_{2}:\quad\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{j}\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,

(3.55)

with analogous reasoning as in (
3.31
) but for
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}})
: The singlet boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
of the trivial SPT will give rise to local operators on the 1d boundary
\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\in\text{End}(\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}})
with
i,j=0,1
as seen below:

\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}
\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}
\psi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{SPT}^{-}}
=
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}

(3.56)

The topological local operators
\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}
will be charged under the anomalous
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry in 1d,

P_{1}:\quad\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{i}\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,\qquad P_{2}:\quad\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{j}\Phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,

(3.57)

with the 1d anomaly
\eta\in H^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))
encoded in the ordering of successively applying the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry action
(P_{1}^{i}P_{2}^{j})\in\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
as

(P_{1}^{i}P_{2}^{j})\otimes(P_{1}^{k}P_{2}^{l})=\eta[(P_{1}^{i}P_{2}^{j}),(P_{1}^{k}P_{2}^{l})]\,\,(P_{1}^{i+k}P_{2}^{j+l})\,.

(3.58)

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}})
: The singlet boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
of the non-trivial SPT will give rise to local operators on the 1d boundary
\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\in\text{End}(\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}})
with
i,j=0,1
as seen below:

\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
\mathsf{Vec}^{+}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0}}
\phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}
\psi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{SPT}^{+}}
=
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{0}}
\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}

(3.59)

The situation is now thus very similar to the previous case
b)
just above where
\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}
is charged under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
as

P_{1}:\quad\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{i}\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,\qquad P_{2}:\quad\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{j}\Phi_{0}^{\hat{P}_{1}^{i}\hat{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,

(3.60)

with the 1d anomaly
\beta\in H^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},U(1))
, encoded in the ordering of successively applying various
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry actions, now coming from the bulk
\text{SPT}^{-}
instead of from the boundary module category
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
.

•

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{+}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{SPT}^{-}})
: Finally, the four boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{m_{0},m_{s,s^{\prime}}}
of the non-trivial SPT transform in a singlet under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and host the following junction operators

\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathsf{Vec}^{-}
m_{0}
m_{0}
m_{(-)^{i}s,(-)^{j}s^{\prime}}
m_{s,s^{\prime}}
\phi_{0}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}
\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}
\psi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{SPT}^{+}}
=
(m_{0},m_{(-)^{i}s,(-)^{j}s^{\prime}})
(m_{0},m_{s,s^{\prime}})
\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}

(3.61)

and they are charged under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
as

P_{1}:\quad\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{i}\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,\qquad P_{2}:\quad\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\to(-)^{j}\Phi_{s,s^{\prime}}^{\bar{P}_{1}^{i}\bar{P}_{2}^{j}}\,,

(3.62)

with the combined boundary symmetry action being anomaly-free.

3.5
Non-Invertible Symmetry:
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})

As an example of a non-anomalous, non-invertible symmetry consider
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
in
(1+1)
d generated by the invertible lines
1
and
P
and the non-invertible line
E
with fusion rules

P\otimes E=E\otimes P=E\,,\quad E\otimes E=1\oplus P\oplus E\,.

(3.63)

The SymTFT description of the gapped phases has appeared in
[
38
]
, which we briefly recap here.
The SymTFT in this case is the (untwisted) 3d
S_{3}
Dijkgraaf-Witten theory
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})
,
6
6

6

This SymTFT can be obtained from the 3d
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
DW gauge theory by gauging the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
outer automorphism which exchanges the pairs
(e,m)\leftrightarrow(e^{2},m^{2})
.
. The associated Drinfeld center has simple lines labeled by conjugacy classes of
S_{3}
(
[\text{id}],[a],[b]
) and the irreducible representations of the centralizers of elements in these conjugacy classes:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}})=\{{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],1},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],P},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],E},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[a],1},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[a],\omega},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[a],\omega^{2}},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[b],+},\,{\bm{Q}}_{[b],-}\}\,,

(3.64)

with
\omega=e^{\pm 2\pi i/3}
.
The Drinfeld center has four Lagrangians given by

\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}&={\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],1}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],P}\oplus 2{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],E}\,,\\
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}&={\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],1}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],E}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[b],+}\,,\end{aligned}\ \qquad\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}&={\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],1}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[a],1}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[b],+}\,,\\
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}&={\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],1}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{[\text{id}],P}\oplus 2{\bm{Q}}_{[a],1}\,,\end{aligned}

(3.65)

where the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{a}
(or equivalently the 2-charge
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{a}
) are labelled by the module category separating
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}
and
\mathcal{L}_{a}
.

By fixing the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}
, we find the four
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
-symmetric gapped phases by varying
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}
of the SymTFT sandwich:

\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}|\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})&=\text{Trivial phase}\,,\\
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}|\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})&=\text{${\mathbb{Z}}_{2}$ SSB Phase}\,,\\
\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}|\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})})&=\text{$\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})$ SSB Phase}\,,\\
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}|\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})&=\text{$\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}$ SSB Phase}\,.\\
\end{aligned}

(3.66)

Module categories.

We have discussed the odule categories in (
2.12
). They are in 1-1 correspondence with Lagrangians. We furthmore indicate how the symmetry
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
acts on these module categories:

•

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}=\mathsf{Vec}=\{m_{0}\}
with trivial
S_{3}
symmetry action and
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
action:

P:\quad m_{0}\to m_{0}\,,\qquad E:\quad m_{0}\to 2m_{0}\,.

(3.67)

•

\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}=\{m_{1},m_{P}\}
with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action

P:\quad m_{1}\leftrightarrow m_{P}\,,\qquad E:\quad m_{1},m_{P}\to m_{1}\oplus m_{P}\,.

(3.68)

•

\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}=\{m_{1},m_{\omega},m_{\omega^{2}}\}
with
S_{3}
symmetry action

a:\quad m_{\omega^{i}}\to m_{\omega^{i+1}}\,,\qquad b:\quad m_{\omega^{i}}\to m_{\omega^{i}}\,,

(3.69)

and
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action

P:\quad m_{\omega^{i}}\to m_{\omega^{i}}\,,\qquad E:\quad m_{\omega^{i}}\to m_{\omega^{i+1}}\oplus m_{\omega^{i+2}}\,.

(3.70)

Notice here that acting with
E
from one side is equivalent to acting with
a+a^{2}
from the other.

•

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}=\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})=\{m_{1},m_{P},m_{E}\}
with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action

\displaystyle P:\quad m_{1}\leftrightarrow m_{P}\,,\quad m_{E}\to m_{E}

(3.71)

\displaystyle E:\quad m_{1},m_{P}\to m_{E}\,,\quad m_{E}\to m_{1}\oplus m_{P}\oplus m_{E}\,.

There is also one more module category that we will need which is the regular module category for
S_{3}
symmetry:

•

\mathcal{M}_{S_{3}}=\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{3}}=\{m_{1},m_{a},m_{a^{2}},m_{b},m_{ab},m_{a^{2}b}\}
with
S_{3}
symmetry action following the standard
S_{3}
group multiplication rules.

Trivial phase.

In the trivial phase, we find one vacuum
v_{0}
and correspondingly one boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
, while the symmetry lines trivialize as

P=1\,,\qquad E=1\oplus 1\,.

(3.72)

By varying
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
we find four Boundary SymTFTs depicted in figure
10
.

\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{S_{3}}
\mathsf{Vec}
{\bf a)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
{\bf b)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
{\bf c)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
{\bf d)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})

Figure 10:
Boundary SymTFTs for the trivial gapped phase with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry.

a)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
: In this phase we find 6 singlet boundary conditions under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. The difference between the BCs is captured in how the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
lines end on the quantum mechanical systems living on the boundary (the 2d phase is trivial so the boundaries are QM systems). Recall that the similar case for non-anomalous
G
symmetry leads to QM systems whose Hilbert spaces are irreducible representations of
G
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be any of these singlet BCs. On all of them the action of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
is

\displaystyle P\otimes\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}

(3.73)

\displaystyle E\otimes\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{B}\oplus\mathfrak{B}\,.

Three of these cases involve the end of
P
line being realized as the number
+1
on the trivial QM
\mathfrak{B}
. These three cases are distinguished by the end of
E
being realized as the map

\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{i}\\
\omega^{2i}\end{pmatrix}:\penalty\ \mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}\oplus\mathfrak{B}\,.

(3.74)

The remaining three cases involve the end of
P
line being realized as the number
-1
on the trivial QM
\mathfrak{B}
. These three cases are distinguished by the end of
E
being realized as the map

\begin{pmatrix}-\omega^{i}\\
\omega^{2i}\end{pmatrix}:\penalty\ \mathfrak{B}\to\mathfrak{B}\oplus\mathfrak{B}\,.

(3.75)

b)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
: There is a triplet of BCs transforming in
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, as the underlying phase is trivial with one boundary invariant
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
, there is symmetry enrichment of the boundary such that there are now three boundaries

\mathfrak{B}^{1,0},\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}=\mathfrak{B}^{0}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}=\mathfrak{B}^{0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{0}\,,

(3.76)

with the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action on these boundaries (or elements of the
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
module category according to (
3.71
)) sending

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,0}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}\,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}\,,

(3.77)

\displaystyle E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,0},\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}\,,

which is consistent with the symmetry action of the trivial phase in (
3.72
). Notice that the boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{E,0}
has to be a composite of two boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
for the symmetry action to be consistent.

c)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
: There are 3 doublets transforming in
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, we can label the boundaries as

\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}=\mathfrak{B}^{0}\,,

(3.78)

where
i=0,1,2
, with symmetry acting only on the first (symmetry) index, with the second labelling different multiplets transforming in the same 1-charge,

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\,,

(3.79)

which is again consistent with (
3.68
) and (
3.72
).
The three doublets are distinguished by the ends of
E
. There are four ends of
E
that we label as
\mathcal{O}_{ab}
for
a,b\in\{1,P\}
. The end
\mathcal{O}_{ab}
takes
\mathfrak{B}^{a,\omega^{i}}
to
\mathfrak{B}^{b,\omega^{i}}
. We have

[\mathcal{O}_{ab}]=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{i}+\omega^{2i}&\omega^{i}-\omega^{2i}\\
\omega^{i}-\omega^{2i}&\omega^{i}+\omega^{2i}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.80)

d)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}})
: Finally, this case is analogous to the one just above but with the roles of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
switched, with us now having two triplets in
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, we can similarly label the boundary elements as

\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},P^{x}}=\mathfrak{B}^{0}\,,

(3.81)

with
i=0,1,2
, and
x=0,1
, with
P^{2}=1
and the symmetry action on these elements

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},P^{x}}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+1},P^{x}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+2},P^{x}}\,,

(3.82)

which is consistent with (
3.70
) and (
3.72
). The two triplets are distinguished by the ends of
P
being
\pm 1
.

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase.

In the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase there are two vacua
v_{1}
and
v_{P}
and correspondingly two irreducible boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
which are exchanged by the broken subsymmetry
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\subset\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, generated by
P
. The non-invertible
E
symmetry line can be identified as

E=1\oplus P\,.

(3.83)

By varying the symmetry module (or equivalently
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
), one can track four cases of Boundary SymTFTs, as shown in figure
11
.

\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
\mathsf{Vec}
{\bf a)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})

\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
{\bf b)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})

{\bf d)}\ (\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})

Figure 11:
Four Boundary SymTFTs for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, with boundary.

a)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})
: This case describes three singlets
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase has two BCs
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
, neither of which is invariant under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. Hence each of the three boundaries in this phase must be a composite boundary

\mathfrak{B}^{0,\omega^{i}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,

(3.84)

The three singlets are distinguished by the ends of
E
along
\mathfrak{B}^{0,\omega^{i}}
which are the same as in (
3.80
).

b)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})
: Here we have two triplets
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
which we can identify in terms of boundaries of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
as

\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{x}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{x}}=\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,

(3.85)

with
x=0,1
, which has consistent
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action with (
3.71
) and (
3.83
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{x}}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{x}}\,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}\,,

(3.86)

\displaystyle E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{x}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{x}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{x}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}\,.

The two triplets are distinguished by the sign of the end of
P
along
\mathfrak{B}^{E,P^{x}}
.

c)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})
: There are 3 doublets transforming naturally under
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
in the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase which we can label

\mathfrak{B}^{1,i}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{P,i}=\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,

(3.87)

with multiplicity given by
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, and natural transformation under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\subset\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
:

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,i}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,i}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,i},\mathfrak{B}^{P,i}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,i}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,i}\,,

(3.88)

which is consistent with (
3.68
) and (
3.83
). These are distinguished by ends of
E
between boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{1,i}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P,i}
. The expressions for these ends is as in (
3.80
).

d)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})
: In this phase, we find a single triplet
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, as the boundaries of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
do not have natural transformation properties under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
or
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
, we find the only possibility for the triplet to be

\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},0}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,

(3.89)

with the enriched symmetry implemented to be consistent with (
3.70
) and (
3.83
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},0}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+1},0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+2},0}\,.

(3.90)

\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase.

In the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase there are three vacua
v_{1}
,
v_{\omega}
and
v_{\omega^{2}}
, and correspondingly three irreducible boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}
which are exchanged by the broken subsymmetry
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}\subset\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, generated by the non-invertible line
E
, whereas the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry generated by
P=1
remains unbroken. One can indeed naturally label the boundaries by
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
irreducible representations
\omega^{i}
for
i=0,1,2
, as the action of
E
in this phase can be expressed using
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
symmetry generators
a
and
a^{2}

E=a\oplus a^{2}:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+1}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+2}}\,,

(3.91)

as discussed previously around (
3.70
).
The four Boundary SymTFTs are shown in figure
12
.

\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}

Figure 12:
Four Boundary SymTFTs describing a (1+1)d
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SSB phase with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry.

a)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
: This case describes two singlets
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase has three BCs
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}
, neither of which is invariant under the full
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. Hence both singlet boundaries in this phase must be composite boundaries

\mathfrak{B}^{0,P^{x}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\,,

(3.92)

for
x=0,1
, and have consistent
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action with (
3.67
) and (
3.91
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{0,P^{x}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{0,P^{x}}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{0,P^{x}}\to 2\mathfrak{B}^{0,P^{x}}\,.

(3.93)

The two singlets are distinguished by the sign of the end of
P
along these BCs.

b)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
: Here we have three triplets
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
which we can identify in terms of boundaries of the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase
\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i}}
as

\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}=\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\,,

(3.94)

which has consistent
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action with (
3.71
) and (
3.91
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\,,

(3.95)

\displaystyle E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\,,

particularly as

\displaystyle E:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\,,

(3.96)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\to 2\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,\omega^{i}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}\,.

The three triplets can be distinguished by studying the ends of
E
line along
\mathfrak{B}^{E,\omega^{i}}
which take the form (
3.80
).

c)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
: In this phase, we find a doublet
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, as the boundaries of the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase
B^{\omega^{i}}
do not have natural transformation properties under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, we find the only possibility for the doublet to be

\mathfrak{B}^{P^{x},0}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2}}\,,

(3.97)

with the enriched symmetry implemented to be consistent with (
3.68
) and (
3.91
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,0}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{P^{x},0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,0}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,0}\,.

(3.98)

d)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}})
: In this phase, we find 18 boundaries transforming naturally in the triplet
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
in the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase which we can label

\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},g}=\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i}}\,,

(3.99)

with
i=0,1,2
, multiplicity given by
g\in S_{3}
, and natural transformation under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\subset\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
consistent with (
3.70
) and (
3.91
):

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},g}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},g}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},g}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+1},g}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+2},g}\,.

(3.100)

\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase.

In the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
phase there are three vacua similarly to the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
SSB phase case but now the remaining
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
is also spontaneously broken leading to physically distinguishable vacua. The corresponding irreducible three boundaries are
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{E}
, labelled by the elements of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
as now these boundaries transform according to the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
fusion rules as in (
3.71
).

By varying the module categories (or equivalently
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
), there are again four cases of Boundary SymTFTs shown in figure
13
).

\mathfrak{Z}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathsf{Vec}
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})})

Figure 13:
Four Boundary SymTFTs for the (1+1)d
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
.

a)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}})
: This case describes a single boundary forming a singlet
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase has three BCs
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{E}
, neither of which is invariant under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. Hence the singlet boundary in this phase must be a composite boundary

\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus 2\mathfrak{B}^{E}\,,

(3.101)

and have consistent
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action with (
3.67
) and (
3.71
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}\to\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}\to 2\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}\,,

(3.102)

particularly as

E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus 2\mathfrak{B}^{E}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\oplus 2(\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E})=2\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}\,.

(3.103)

The form of the singlet
\mathfrak{B}^{0,0}
in (
3.101
) is not accidental as it represents the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
which is the symmetry boundary of the SymTFT for
S_{3}
symmetry.

b)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}})
: Here we have a set of 9 boundaries transforming naturally in the triplet
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
which we can label as

\mathfrak{B}^{R,R^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{B}^{R}\,,

(3.104)

for
R,R^{\prime}=1,P,E
, which is consistent with the natural
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action of (
3.71
)

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,R^{\prime}}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,R^{\prime}}\,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,R^{\prime}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,R^{\prime}}\,,

(3.105)

\displaystyle E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,R^{\prime}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,R^{\prime}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E,R^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{E,R^{\prime}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,R^{\prime}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,R^{\prime}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E,R^{\prime}}\,.

c)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})})
: In this phase, we find four boundaries transforming in the doublet
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, even though the boundaries
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
of the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase have natural transformation properties under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, the last boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{E}
is an odd man out as it is invariant under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. We find the only possibility for the doublet to be

\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}=\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\,,

(3.106)

with multiplicity given by
y=0,1
, and the symmetry action consistent with (
3.68
) and (
3.71
) to be

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}},\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}\,.

(3.107)

Particularly,
P
transformation is evident as
\mathfrak{B}^{1}\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{B}^{P}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{E}
is invariant, whereas
E
is again a bit less clear:

E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\to\mathfrak{B}^{E}\oplus(\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E})\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}\,,

(3.108)

and similarly for
\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}
.

Notice again that in fact combining the doublet into a singlet under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry produces the result in (
3.101
), i.e.

\mathfrak{B}^{1,P^{y}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P,P^{y}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus 2\mathfrak{B}^{E}\,,

(3.109)

which is the canonical Lagrangian algebra for
S_{3}
symmetry, invariant under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
.

d)

(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})}\mid\mathcal{L}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}})
: Finally, in this case, we find 9 boundaries transforming in the triplet
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
. However, as the boundaries of the
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
SSB phase
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
,
\mathfrak{B}^{P}
, do not have natural transformation properties under
{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
or
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})/Z_{2}
, we find the triplet to be

\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{j}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\,,\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega,\omega^{j}},\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2},\omega^{j}}=\mathfrak{B}^{E}\,,

(3.110)

with multiplicity
j=0,1,2
, and consistent symmetry action with (
3.70
) and (
3.71
) as

\displaystyle P:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},\omega^{j}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},\omega^{j}}\,,\qquad E:\quad\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i},\omega^{j}}\to\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+1},\omega^{j}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{i+2},\omega^{j}}\,,

(3.111)

which particularly for the
E
action can be seen as

\displaystyle E:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{j}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\to 2\mathfrak{B}^{E}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{\omega,\omega^{j}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2},\omega^{j}}\,,

(3.112)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\omega,\omega^{j}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{E}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{j}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2},\omega^{j}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}^{\omega^{2},\omega^{j}}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{E}\to\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{E}\sim\mathfrak{B}^{1,\omega^{j}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{\omega,\omega^{j}}\,.

Boundary-changing operators.

Rather than going over all 16 cases mentioned above with various multiplicities, let us concentrate on a few interesting examples to showcase the general characteristics of boundary-changing operators for boundary phases with non-invertible symmetry. We can organize these based on the module category
\mathcal{M}
which is acted on from the left by the symmetry lines, which forms the top left corner of the Boundary SymTFT:

•

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
: In this case, all six
S_{3}
symmetry lines can end on
\mathcal{M}
from the right or more precisely on the singlet boundary
m_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}
. If we label these junctions on which the topological lines
a^{i}b^{x}
, with
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
,
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, can end as
\phi^{a^{i}b^{x}}
then one can show these junctions transform under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
from the left as

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:\quad\phi^{a^{i}}\to\phi^{a^{i}}\,,\qquad\phi^{b}\to-\phi^{b}\qquad

(3.113)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle:\quad\phi^{a}\to\omega\phi^{a}\oplus\omega^{2}\phi^{a^{2}}\,,\qquad\phi^{a^{2}}\to\omega^{2}\phi^{a}\oplus\omega\phi^{a^{2}}\,,\qquad\phi^{b}\to 0\,,\qquad\phi^{1}\to 2\phi^{1}\,.

This is consistent with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action on topological local operators found in
[
38
]
, i.e.
combining these junctions based on conjugacy classes as

\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}=\phi^{a}\oplus\phi^{a^{2}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{O}^{b}=\phi^{b}\oplus\phi^{ab}\oplus\phi^{ab^{2}}\,,

(3.114)

one finds the same symmetry action

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:\quad\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}\to\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}\,,\qquad\mathcal{O}^{b}\to-\mathcal{O}^{b}\,,

(3.115)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle:\quad\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}\to-\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}\,,\qquad\mathcal{O}^{b}\to 0\,,

as
\omega+\omega^{2}=-1
.

The action (
3.113
) is interesting, especially as the non-invertible line
E
maps, for example,
\phi^{a}\to\omega\phi^{a}\oplus\omega^{2}\phi^{a^{2}}
, thus if we take this less general example where we fix our initial boundary to be
\mathfrak{B}^{0,1}
then the picture is

\mathcal{M}_{S_{3}}
\mathsf{Vec}
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
\mathfrak{B}^{a}
\mathfrak{B}^{1}
\phi^{a}
a
\psi_{1,a}^{a}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
=
\mathfrak{B}^{0,a}
\mathfrak{B}^{0,1}
\Phi_{1,a}^{a}

(3.116)

Then we find that the boundary-changing operator
\Phi_{1,a}^{a}
transforms under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
as

P:\quad\Phi_{1,a}^{a}\to\Phi_{1,a}^{a}\,,\qquad E:\quad\Phi_{1,a}^{a}\to\omega\Phi_{1,a}^{a}\oplus\omega^{2}\Phi_{1,a^{2}}^{a^{2}}\,,

(3.117)

hence we see that even though the boundaries are in a singlet under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
, the non-invertible symmetry can still transform the boundary through the action on the boundary-changing operator, which in turn can even act on the non-symmetry index, which is not possible in invertible cases.

•

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}}
: In this case, only the
[a]=(a\oplus a^{2})_{S_{3}}
symmetry line (of
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})^{\prime}
) can end on
\mathcal{M}
from the right or more precisely on the doublet of boundaries
m_{1},m_{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
. If we label these junctions on which the topological line
[a]
can end as
\phi^{[a]}
then one can show this junctions transform under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
from the left as

P:\quad\phi^{[a]}\to\phi^{[a]}\,,\qquad E:\quad\phi^{[a]}\to-\phi^{[a]}\,,

(3.118)

which is consistent with
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry action on topological local operators by simply identifying
\phi^{[a]}=\mathcal{O}^{a}_{+}
.

•

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}}
: In this case, only the
b
symmetry line (of
S_{3}^{\prime}
) can end on
\mathcal{M}
from the right or more precisely on the triplet of boundaries
m_{1},m_{\omega},m_{\omega^{2}}\in\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}
. If we label this junction on which the topological line
b
can end as
\phi^{b}
as previously then one can show this junction transforms under
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
from the left as

P:\quad\phi^{b}\to-\phi^{b}\,,\qquad E:\quad\phi^{b}\to 0\,,

(3.119)

which is consistent with what we have already seen for
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
.

•

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
: This is just the case of having the boundaries transform naturally in the regular module category where no symmetry lines can end completely from the right on
\mathcal{M}
, hence the boundary-changing operators will transform under the standard
\mathsf{Rep}(S_{3})
symmetry rules.

3.6
Interfaces between SPTs

As we have alluded in the Introduction, our formalism also allows the description of symmetric interfaces between phases, which we will denote as
\mathcal{I}
.
Here by symmetric, we mean interfaces which are transparent to the symmetry action, as depicted in figure
14
.

\mathcal{I}_{12}
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
D_{1}
=
\mathcal{I}_{12}
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
D_{1}
=
\mathcal{I}_{12}
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
D_{1}
\mathcal{I}_{12}
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
D_{1}^{\prime}
D_{1}^{\prime\prime}
D_{1}
=\,\beta_{D_{1},\,D_{1}^{\prime}}^{D_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
\mathcal{I}_{12}
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
D_{1}^{\prime}
D_{1}^{\prime\prime}
D_{1}

Figure 14:
Above, symmetry defect
D_{1}
can be freely moved through a symmetric interface
\mathcal{I}_{12}
between two phases
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
. Below, composition law for symmetry defects along the interface.

After folding, the symmetric interface is described by the following boundary sandwich:

\mathfrak{T}_{1}
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
\mathcal{I}
=
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{1}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{2}}
\mathcal{I}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
=
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{S}}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{1}\boxtimes\mathfrak{T}_{2}}
\mathcal{I}
M
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{diag}}

(3.120)

Where
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\text{diag}}
is the gapped BC corresponding to the universal diagonal Lagrangian algebra in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})}

[
79
]
.
Further interfaces can be constructed from codimension-1 topological defects in
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
which leave the gapped BC
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
invariant by simply stretching them through the bulk. We do not study these objects in this work.

Of special interest to us will be interfaces between gapped phases and, in particular, between invertible phases (SPTs).
For invertible symmetry, such problem has a long history, while for non-invertible ones –especially for Tambara-Yamagami categories– an illuminating early study is
[
3
]
, while a recent lattice construction is
[
80
]
.
Of interest to use will be two key observables

•

The associator for the symmetry defects on the interface. In the folded picture, these are the boundary F-symbols for the diagonal symmetry defects
D_{1}^{S\otimes\bar{S}}
.

•

The number of edge modes on the interface --that is-- the ground-state degeneracy of the interface Hilbert space.
7
7

7

This is defined by taking time to run in the vertical direction along the interface. Notice also that, in lattice constructions, one considers a periodic system with gapped phases
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
on the two halves of the circle. In this way has two insertions of the interface and the number of edge modes squares.
Recall that, for an SPT for a discrete group, the symmetry acts projectively on the 1d boundary, so the interface Hilbert space is always degenerate. Similarly, the nontriviality of the boundary F-symbols often forbids a nondegenerate interface Hilbert space.

The first observable has essentially been described in
[
24
]
, for invertible symmetries. The main idea is the following: since the theories on bot sides of the interface are SPTs, the symmetry defect can be trivialized. That is:

(3.121)

The symmetry action then localizes on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{12}
and its algebra can be computed via the bulk data.
To understand edge modes, we must give a construction of the interface Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{I}}
, on which the operators described above act. As usual in TFT, this is obtained by “closing” the (2+1)d bulk geometry (
3.120
). This gives rise to the following junction between gapped boundaries:

\mathcal{M}_{2}
\mathcal{M}_{1}
\mathcal{I}_{12}
V_{\mathcal{I}_{12}}

(3.122)

The three-valent junction
V_{\mathcal{I}_{12}}
is a vector space, which is isomorphic to the interface Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{I}_{12}}
8
8

8

More precisely, to its ground states.
.
It’s dimension is in general hard to compute, but can sometimes be extracted from the description of
\mathcal{M}_{1},\,\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\mathcal{I}_{12}
as gauging interfaces using the techniques of
[
81
]
.
We will now compute these data in two classes of examples, showcasing the power of the SymTFT.

SPTs for discrete Groups

For simplicity we will study Abelian symmetries
9
9

9

For this kind of symmetries, adge modes have also been studied in
[
24
]
using the SymTFT formulation.
, the non-abelian generalization is also straightforward but tedious. We denote the underlying group by
G
. SPTs are classified by classes
\omega\,\in\,H^{2}(G,U(1))
. These are most easily described via their associated anti-symmetric bicharacter

\chi_{\omega}(a,b)=\frac{\omega(a,b)}{\omega(b,a)}\,,

(3.123)

which encodes the gauge-invariant information about
\omega
. Let us consider an interface between two SPTs, say SPT
1
and SPT
2
, described by classes
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
. By folding we obtain an interface between the SPT described by
\omega_{1}/\omega_{2}
and the trivial theory.
As discussed in section
2.2
, the symmetry action on the boundary is labelled by the boundary F-symbol:

\hat{F}_{a,b}=\frac{\omega_{1}(a,b)}{\omega_{2}(a,b)}\,,

(3.124)

alternatively, the algebra of lines on the interface is:

D_{1}^{a}\otimes D_{1}^{b}=\frac{\chi_{1}(a,b)}{\chi_{2}(a,b)}\,D_{1}^{b}\otimes D_{1}^{a}\,.

(3.125)

Let us see how this is encoded in the SymTFT. We will follow the conventions of
[
39
,
43
]
and denote a bulk anyon by
(a,\,\alpha)\,\in\,G\times G^{\vee}
.
The symmetry boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
is described by the magnetic algebra:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\,(0,\alpha)\,.

(3.126)

On the other hand, the SPTs are described by gapped BC
[
70
,
39
]
:

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\bigoplus_{a}\,(a,\,\psi_{\omega}(a))\,,

(3.127)

where
\psi_{\omega}:\,G\to G^{\vee}
is an invertible group homomorphism such that:

\psi_{\omega}(a)[b]=\chi_{\omega}(a,b)\,.

(3.128)

In order to compute the commutator in the SymTFT we employ the following representation of the symmetry lines crossing the interface:

\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{2}}
\mathcal{I}
D_{1}^{b}
D_{1}^{a}
(b,\psi_{\omega_{1}}(b))
(a,\psi_{\omega_{1}}(a))
\overline{(b,\psi_{\omega_{2}}(b))}
\overline{(a,\psi_{\omega_{2}}(a))}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}

(3.129)

Shrinking the
D_{1}^{a}
line we obtain the anyon
(0,\psi_{\omega_{1}}(a)-\psi_{\omega_{2}}(a))

stretching between the interface and the
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
boundary:

\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{2}}
\mathcal{I}
D_{1}^{a}
(a,\psi_{\omega_{1}}(a))
\overline{(a,\psi_{\omega_{2}}(a))}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
=
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{2}}
\mathcal{I}
(0,\psi_{\omega_{1}}(a)-\psi_{\omega_{2}}(a))
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}

(3.130)

Commuting this with the
D_{1}^{b}
line gives a phase:

\frac{\psi_{\omega_{1}}(a)[b]}{\psi_{\omega_{2}}(a)[b]}=\frac{\chi_{\omega_{1}}(a,b)}{\chi_{\omega_{2}}(a,b)}\,,

(3.131)

reproducing the known QFT answer.
Let us now focus on
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, for which the interface Hilbert space is two-fold degenerate. The interfaces
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
implement the gauging of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, with or without discrete torsion, respectively. The same is true for
\mathcal{I}_{12}
. Since all three module categories have a single element the fusion of interfaces must be of the form:

\mathcal{M}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{2}=\text{dim}(V_{\mathcal{I}_{12}})\,\mathcal{I}_{12}\,,

(3.132)

following
[
81
]
, notice that the quantum dimension on any gauging interface is:

d_{\mathcal{I}}=\sqrt{\text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{I}})}\,,

(3.133)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{I}}
is the algebra object implementing the generalized gauging. In our case
\text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}})=\text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{M}_{2}})=\text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{I}_{12}})=4
, so we conclude:

\text{dim}(V_{\mathcal{I}_{12}})=2\,,

(3.134)

recovering the expected counting of edge modes.

Non-invertible symmetry:
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})

The paramount example of nontrivial interfaces between SPTs is given by the three SPTs under
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
symmetry, which we dub:

\text{SPT}_{R},\,\text{SPT}_{G},\,\text{SPT}_{B}\,.

(3.135)

We will denote invertible elements
g\,\in\,\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
by
P_{1},\,P_{2},\,P_{1}P_{2}
while
E
will denote the non-invertible object, satisfying:

E^{2}=1+P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{1}P_{2}\,.

(3.136)

At the level of the invertible
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
subcategory of
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
, the three SPTs are indistinguishable and identified with the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
SPT. They can however be distinguished via the action of the non-invertible element
E
, given by 1-cochain
\nu
satisfying
[
3
]
:

\displaystyle\nu(g)^{2}=1\,,\ \ \ g\,\in\,\left\{P_{1},\,P_{2},\,P_{1}P_{2}\right\}

(3.137)

\displaystyle\nu(P_{1})\nu(P_{2})\nu(P_{1}P_{1})=-1\,.

(3.138)

the three numbers
\nu(P_{1}),\,\nu(P_{2}),\,\nu(P_{1}P_{2})
furthermore need to satisfy:

\text{sign}\left(1+\nu(P_{1})+\nu(P_{2})+\nu(P_{1}P_{2})\right)=1\,.

(3.139)

The solution is to assign a
-
sign an element of the list
P_{1},\,P_{2},\,P_{1}P_{2}
describing three cochains
\nu_{R},\,\nu_{G},\,\nu_{B}
.
The interface between two such SPTs
i
and
j
is characterized by the ratio of two co-chains, which we denote by
\nu_{ij}\equiv\nu_{i}/\nu_{j}
whose values are:

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}&P_{1}&P_{2}&P_{1}P_{2}\\
\hline\cr\nu_{RG}&-&-&+\\
\nu_{GB}&+&-&-\\
\nu_{RB}&-&+&-\end{array}

(3.140)

We will now recover this characterization from the SymTFT.
Following the notation of
[
45
]
–to which we also refer the reader for further details about the structure of the Drinfeld center– the relevant Lagrangian algebras are:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}=1\oplus e_{RGB}\oplus m_{GB}\oplus m_{RB}\oplus m_{RG}\,,

(3.141)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{R}=1\oplus e_{B}\oplus e_{G}\oplus e_{BG}\oplus 2m_{R}\,,

(3.142)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{G}=1\oplus e_{B}\oplus e_{R}\oplus e_{RB}\oplus 2m_{G}\,,

(3.143)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{B}=1\oplus e_{R}\oplus e_{G}\oplus e_{RG}\oplus 2m_{B}\,.

(3.144)

The one-cochain can be extracted from the commutation relations between an invertible line and the non-invertible generator
E
:

D_{1}^{g}\otimes D_{1}^{E}=\nu_{ij}(g)D_{1}^{E}\otimes D_{1}^{g}\,.

(3.145)

We will focus on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
between
\text{SPT}_{R}
and
\text{SPT}_{G}
. The other interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{RB}
and
\mathcal{I}_{GB}
are obtained by permuting the
RGB
labels.
For this choice of interface the description of the symmetry action is as follows

\mathcal{L}_{R}
\mathcal{L}_{G}
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
D_{1}^{E}
\left\{D_{1}^{P_{1}},\,D_{1}^{P_{2}},\,D_{1}^{P_{1}P_{2}}\right\}
m_{R}
\left\{e_{BG},\,e_{G},\,e_{B}\right\}
m_{G}
\left\{e_{R},\,e_{RB},\,e_{B}\right\}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}

(3.146)

Shrinking the upper line triplet
\left\{D_{1}^{P_{1}},\,D_{1}^{P_{2}},\,D_{1}^{P_{1}P_{2}}\right\}
gives rise to
\left\{e_{RGB},\,e_{RGB},\,1\right\}
:

\mathcal{L}_{R}
\mathcal{L}_{G}
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
\left\{D_{1}^{P_{1}},\,D_{1}^{P_{2}},\,D_{1}^{P_{1}P_{2}}\right\}
\left\{e_{BG},\,e_{G},\,e_{B}\right\}
\left\{e_{R},\,e_{RB},\,e_{B}\right\}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
=
\mathcal{L}_{R}
\mathcal{L}_{G}
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
\left\{e_{RGB},\,e_{RGB},\,1\right\}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}

(3.147)

Following
[
45
]
, the endpoint of the
e_{RGB}
line is charged under the
D_{1}^{E}
symmetry, thus we find:

D_{1}^{g}\otimes D_{1}^{E}=\nu_{ij}(g)\,D_{1}^{E}\otimes D_{1}^{g}\,,

(3.148)

as expected. Cycling through the three interfaces we recover Table (
3.140
).
Let us now describe the counting of edge modes. Since:

\mathcal{L}_{R}\cap\mathcal{L}_{G}=1\oplus e_{B}\,,

(3.149)

there are two (physically equivalent) choices for the interface
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
, let us denote then by
\mathcal{I}_{RG}^{\pm}
. Furthermore, since we are describing Fiber-Functors, it is known
[
81
]
that the algebras
\mathcal{A}_{R,G}
take the “maximal” form:

\mathcal{A}_{R,G}=1+P_{1}+P_{1}+P_{1}P_{2}+2E\,,\ \ \ \ \text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{R,G})=8\,.

(3.150)

Furthermore,
\mathcal{I}_{RG}
implements the gauging of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
10
10

10

A way to understand this is that
\mathsf{Rep}(D_{8})
is Morita equivalent to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{R}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{G}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{B}
symmetry with ’t Hooft anomaly
A_{R}A_{G}A_{B}
. The gapped BC corresponding to the three SPTs can be reached from the
Dir
boundary condition for this symmetry by gauging a single
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. This follows e.g. from the discussion in
[
80
]
, or can be derived directly. Thus to connect any two of these boundaries we must gauge a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry without discrete torsion, where one of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
s is a magnetic symmetry.
, so
\text{dim}(\mathcal{A}_{RG})=4
. The fusion rule now takes the form:

\mathcal{M}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{G}=\text{dim}(V_{\mathcal{I}_{RG}})\,\left(\mathcal{I}_{RG}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{I}_{RG}^{-}\right)\,,

(3.151)

plugging in the numbers we again find:

\text{dim}(V_{\mathcal{I}_{RG}})=2\,,

(3.152)

in accordance with the results of
[
80
]
.

Of course, a more general exploration of these phenomena, especially in higher dimensions, would be of great interest. We believe the SymTFT to be the perfect tool for this.

3.7
Boundary Club Sandwich: Gapless Phases

General Boundary SymTFT description

In the presence of categorical symmetries, (1+1)d gapless phases, describing critical points between two gapped phases, have a SymTFT description as well. More precisely, we can construct associated KT transformations, which transform a phase transition for a symmetry
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
to one for a larger symmetry
\mathcal{S}
. In this way, only
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
acts on the gapless degrees of freedom.
This setup, also known as the “club sandwich” is defined by a symmetry and a physical boundary, as well as an interface between the topological order
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. This is given in terms of a condensable –but not Lagrangian– algebra
\mathcal{A}
in the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
:

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
\mathcal{A}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})

(3.153)

Gapless SPT (gSPT) phases are of particular interest. These are gapless phases, with a unique ground state on a circle on which only
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
acts faithfully on the gapless degrees of freedom. They are characterized by condensable algebras
\mathcal{A}_{\text{gSPT}}
, such that

\mathcal{A}_{\text{gSPT}}\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}=1\,.

(3.154)

If a gSPT cannot be deformed to an SPT phase without breaking the symmetry, it is called an igSPT. These are particularly interesting gapless phases, as they correspond to symmetry-protected critical points. There are examples with group and non-invertible symmetries
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
,
58
,
59
,
24
,
25
,
60
,
45
]
as well as in higher dimensions
[
42
,
43
]
.
Typically such phases are reached via RG flow from a UV,
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric theory
\mathfrak{T}^{UV}
. If we keep in mind such picture, it is natural to ask how the boundary condition multiplets
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathcal{M}}
in the UV are mapped to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-symmetric multiplets
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
. This is not a trivial problem. Indeed, even for flows between unitary minimal models, the complete map between boundary conditions between the UV and IR is only understood for certain RG flows
[
82
,
83
,
84
]
.

Club-Sandwich with Boundary.

Adding boundaries to the club sandwich is described as follows: again there is a
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
charge that characterizes the boundary condition of the SymTFT:

\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{phys}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
\mathcal{M}
M
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{I}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}_{\text{low}})

(3.155)

and
\mathcal{M}
is again a module category. The
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-symmetric boundary condition is instead described by a 1-charge
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
. The interface
\mathcal{I}
is a trivalent junction between
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
,
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}
.
Given
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
and
\mathcal{A}
, not all the charges
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
are allowed to appear in the IR. To this end consider the parallel fusion:

{\bm{Q}}_{2}
\mathcal{A}
=\,\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}n_{\mathcal{A}\,{\bm{Q}}_{2}}^{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}

(3.156)

The integer
n_{\mathcal{A}\,{\bm{Q}}_{2}}^{Q_{2}^{\prime}}
counts the number of independent boundary interfaces
\mathcal{I}
, and the allowed choices of
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
are those for which
n_{\mathcal{A}\,{\bm{Q}}_{2}}^{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}
is non-vanishing.

The club sandwich has an equivalent description in terms of the gapped boundary condition via the folding trick:

{\bm{Q}}_{2}
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}
\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{I}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
=
{\bm{Q}}_{2}\boxtimes\overline{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}
\mathcal{I}

(3.157)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a Lagrangian algebra of the folded topological order
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})\boxtimes\overline{\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})}
, which is a lift of the algebra
\mathcal{A}
(see
[
44
]
for details). In particular in
\mathcal{I}
is a module category for this folded setup, describing the interface between two gapped boundary conditions
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\bm{Q}}_{2}\boxtimes\overline{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}
.
The physical properties of
\mathcal{I}
, allow for instance the determination of the mapping between boundary conditions and boundary changing operators from the UV,
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric theory, into the IR.

Mapping between BC: UV to IR

Consider an
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric gapless RG flow. Typically the faithfully acting symmetry in the IR fits in a group extension

1\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\text{gap}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\longrightarrow 1\,,

(3.158)

where
\mathcal{S}
is the full symmetry of the UV theory–which for simplicity we take to be a group– and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
the quotient acting on the gapless sector. This picture can be extended to arbitrary fusion categories using the SymTFT picture we introduce below.

We now consider a UV,
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}^{UV}
associated to a
\mathcal{S}
module category
\mathcal{M}
. Its image in the
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-symmetric theory can be extracted by pushing the
\mathcal{I}
interface of (
3.155
) onto
\mathcal{M}
.
The interface
\mathcal{I}
thus provides a map between the set of UV boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{UV}
and the set of IR boundary conditions
\mathfrak{B}^{IR}
, in much the same way as the bulk interface
\mathcal{A}
provides a map between
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-symmetric QFTs.
In typical RG flows, the set of IR boundary conditions is strictly smaller than the set of UV boundary conditions, so the map
\mathcal{I}
is not injective. We will see, however, that the opposite phenomenon can also take place: a single UV boundary condition can become non-simple in the IR and decompose into a multiplet under
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. This in particular happens if the RG flow describes an intrinsically gapless SPT (igSPT) phase. This can arise if the group extension (
3.158
) is non-trivial. We will give an explicit example of this phenomenon, together with its physical interpretation, in the examples below.

Example: gSPT and gSSB for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}
\mathcal{I}_{a}
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{4})
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\bf a)}\ \ (\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\mid\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}\mid
e^{2}
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
=
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{a}
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
\mathcal{O}^{-}
\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}
\mathcal{I}_{c}
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{4})
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
{\bf b)}\ \ (\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\mid\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}\mid
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
=
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{b}
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}

Figure 15:
Boundary SymTFTs for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
-symmetric irreducible (non-intrinsic) gapless phases. Diagram a) denotes
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
gSSB phase with two universes while diagrams b) denotes
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
gSPT phase.

A standard and simple example is to consider
\mathcal{S}={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, with a trivial ’t Hooft anomaly, where we will denote the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
symmetry generator as
P
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry generator as
P^{\prime}
. This is achieved by condensing the bulk algebras

\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}=1\oplus e^{2}\,,\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}=1\oplus m^{2}\,,

(3.159)

while fixing the symmetry boundary

\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}=1\oplus e\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}\,.

(3.160)

Condensing the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}
is equivalent to describing a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
gSSB phase for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
symmetry whereas condensing the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}
amounts to depicting a gSPT phase
[
44
,
45
]
.

Let us determine the fate of the regular
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
module category under RG flow. For this we must choose

{\bm{Q}}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\,,

(3.161)

and the two possible maps are determined by the IR multiples given by the choice of either condensing
\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}
or
\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}
:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}=2\mathcal{A}_{e^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{e^{\prime}}\,,

(3.162)

where the factor of 2 tells us there are two inequivalent choices of interface for
\mathcal{I}_{a}
in contrast to
\mathcal{I}_{b}
which only has one.

Starting with
\mathcal{A}_{e^{2}}
, the bulk theory splits into two
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-symmetric universes
(+)
and
(-)
in the IR, exchanged by the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
symmetry line
P\sim m
(projection of
m
on
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
), denoting a gSSB phase.
In each of these universes we have a doublet of boundary conditions for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry
P^{\prime}
which is a projection of the bulk line
m^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{L}_{e^{\prime}}
or equivalently of
m^{2}
on
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}
,
P^{\prime}\sim m^{\prime}\sim m^{2}
. The map
\mathcal{I}_{a}:\,\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{a}
is described by

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{+}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{+}=\mathfrak{B}^{m^{2}}\,,

(3.163)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{-}=\mathfrak{B}^{m}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{-}=\mathfrak{B}^{m^{3}}\,,

with the symmetry action

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle:\quad(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{+}\to(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{-}\to(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{+}\to(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{-}\to(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{+}\,,

(3.164)

\displaystyle P^{2}=P^{\prime}

\displaystyle:\quad(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{+}\leftrightarrow(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{+}\,,\qquad(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})_{-}\leftrightarrow(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})_{-}\,.

We can clearly see that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{a}\simeq(\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})_{+}\oplus(\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})_{-}
with
P^{2}=P^{\prime}
being the natural action on the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
module category
(\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})_{\pm}
and
P
providing a map between the two copies of
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
.

Considering instead
\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}}
, the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetry line
P^{\prime}
acts trivially in the IR of the single universe describing a gSPT phase and the regular representations map between each other through
\mathcal{I}_{b}:\,\mathcal{M}_{\text{reg}}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{b}
as

\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{B}^{1}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{m^{2}}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{B}^{m}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{m^{3}}\,,

(3.165)

with
P
symmetry action exchanging the two boundary conditions as

P:\quad(\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}})\leftrightarrow(\mathfrak{B}^{m^{\prime}})\,.

(3.166)

We can thus see that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{b}\simeq\mathcal{M}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}
with
P
providing the natural action on the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
module category.

Example: igSPT for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\omega}
.

\mathsf{Vec}
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}
\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}
\mathcal{A}
\mathcal{I}
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{4})
\mathfrak{Z}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\omega})
(\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}\mid\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}\mid\mathcal{A}\mid\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}\mid
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}

Figure 16:
Boundary SymTFT for the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
-symmetric intrinsically gapless phase (igSPT).

An enticing example of the splitting of boundary conditions along RG flows comes by considering a flow from a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
-symmetric system to a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{\omega}
-symmetric one with nontrivial ’t Hooft anomaly, implementing the non-trivial
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
igSPT.
In this case the condensable algebra is
\mathcal{A}=1\oplus e^{2}m^{2}
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
is the double semion model, whose nontrivial lines we again denote (as in section
3.4.2
) by
s
and
\bar{s}
, respectively. This model has a unique gapped boundary condition:
\mathcal{L}_{s\bar{s}}=1+s\bar{s}
.
The symmetry boundary for
{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\text{reg}}=1\oplus e\oplus e^{2}\oplus e^{3}
and we choose the boundary
{\bm{Q}}_{2}
to be the singlet
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Vec}}=1\oplus m\oplus m^{2}\oplus m^{3}
.
The module category
\mathcal{M}
is then given by
\mathsf{Vec}
, with a single boundary state
\mathfrak{B}^{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus s\bar{s}

(3.167)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=1\oplus e^{2}m^{2}\oplus(em^{3}+e^{3}m)s\oplus(em+e^{3}m^{3})\bar{s}\oplus(e^{2}+m^{2})s\bar{s}\,,

or, in the folded picture

\mathcal{L}_{{\bm{Q}}_{2}\boxtimes\overline{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}}=(1\oplus m\oplus m^{2}\oplus m^{3})(1\oplus s\bar{s})\,.

(3.168)

The intersection between
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{A}}\cap\mathcal{L}_{{\bm{Q}}_{2}\boxtimes\overline{{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{\prime}}}=1\oplus m^{2}s\bar{s}
gives rise to a module category of two indecomposable objects, describing a doublet of boundary conditions in the IR.
Explicitly, the map
\mathcal{I}
is

\mathfrak{B}^{0}=\mathfrak{B}^{1^{\prime}}\oplus\mathfrak{B}^{P^{\prime}}\,,

(3.169)

with the UV
P^{\prime}=P^{2}
line responsible for the splitting.
This set of ideas can be developed in fuller generality and hopefully allow to address interesting physical boundary RG flows between (1+1)d symmetric CFTs. A prime candidate are current-current deformations of WZW models, whose rich phenomenology has been studied using integrability methods
[
85
]
.

An explanation for the splitting

The splitting of BC can be given an enticing physical interpretation in our setup. Consider, in the UV, a symmetry line
D_{1}^{S}
,
S\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}
, ending on the singlet BC, thus defining a topological junction
\varphi^{S}_{UV}
.
We now assume that
S\,\in\,\mathcal{S}_{\text{gap}}
, so that it does not act on the gapless IR degrees of freedom. Under RG flow the topological junction
\varphi^{S}_{UV}
is mapped to a (possibly trivial) topological local boundary operator
\varphi_{IR}^{S}
:

\mathfrak{B}^{UV}
\mathfrak{T}^{UV}
S
\varphi^{S}_{UV}
\leadsto
\mathfrak{B}^{IR}
\mathfrak{T}^{IR}
\varphi^{S}_{IR}

(3.170)

We conclude that, from the IR perspective, the boundary condition
\mathfrak{B}_{\partial}^{IR}
can split in many universes, depending on whether or not
\varphi^{S}_{IR}
is trivial.
The precise functor
\mathcal{I}^{UV}_{IR}
implementing the mapping can be extracted from the SymTFT by studying the interface
\mathcal{I}
described above, and in particular its number of indecomposable components.
In the case of an igSPT we have shown that the interface
\mathcal{I}
has two components, even if the original module cateogy has a single simple object.
Indeed, as the IR symmetry
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
has no Fiber Functor, we expect that
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathcal{M}}
must split. This is a consequence of the fact that an anomalous symmetry does not admit an invariant BC
[
86
,
48
]
.
This immediately follows from the SymTFT picture, as for anomalous symmetries the module category
\mathcal{M}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}}\mathcal{I}
cannot have a single object due to the
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
’t Hooft anomaly.

4
(1+1)d Lattice Models with Boundary Conditions

(1+1)d lattice models with fusion category symmetries
\mathcal{S}
provide a UV description of categorical phases. The anyon chain models
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
]
provide a systematic way to construct such lattice models with any fusion category symmetry
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, the anyon chain allows for a systematic construction of Hamiltonians, whose ground states realize
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric phases – gapped and gapless
[
69
]
.
Here we discuss how they can be extended to construct lattice models with boundary conditions transforming in arbitrary 1-charges of
\mathcal{S}
.

Let us first review the construction of the anyonic chain with periodic boundary conditions following
[
69
]
, which we will then extend to anyonic chain on a segment. The construction begins with an input fusion category
\mathcal{C}
11
11

11

This is not the symmetry category!
and an input module category
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{C}
such that the dual fusion category formed by
\mathcal{C}
-endofunctors of
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}
, is the symmetry fusion category
\mathcal{S}
that one wants to consider, i.e.

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}}\,.

(4.1)

One moreover chooses an arbitrary object
\rho\in\mathcal{C}
that is not necessarily simple. The data so far fixes the Hilbert space of the model, with a canonical basis specified by all possible configurations of the form

(4.2)

where
m_{i}
run over arbitrary simple objects of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mu_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
run over arbitrary morphisms in a fixed basis of
\text{Hom}(m_{i},\rho\otimes m_{i+1})\in\mathcal{M}
. As the system is on a circle, the
m_{1}
on the left is identified with the one on the right.

The Hamiltonian of the (1+1)d system is specified by choosing a morphism

h:\penalty\ \rho\otimes\rho\to\rho\otimes\rho\,,

(4.3)

which acts on two adjacent sites as

(4.4)

The full Hamiltonian is obtained by summing over the above map over all pairs of sites.

In the pictures of the model drawn above,
\mathcal{C}
acts on
\mathcal{M}
from the top. Correspondingly,
\mathcal{S}
acts on
\mathcal{M}
from the bottom. That is, the action of a symmetry
s\in\mathcal{S}
on the Hilbert space of the model is obtained simply by fusing the
s
line into
(m_{i},\mu_{i+\frac{1}{2}})
in the figure below

(4.5)

The fact that the action of symmetry commutes with the Hamiltonian is encoded in the fact that they act on
\mathcal{M}
from the opposite (bottom and top respectively) directions.

Boundary Conditions.

We can include boundary conditions by defining the model on a segment rather than a circle. Let us say we want to have a BC transforming in 1-charge
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{L}
on the left, and a BC transforming in 1-charge
{\bm{Q}}_{2}^{R}
on the right. These correspond respectively to some module categories
\mathcal{M}_{L}
and
\mathcal{M}_{R}
of
\mathcal{S}
. In particular, let us say that we want to construct a BC corresponding to a (not necessarily simple) object
m_{L}\in\mathcal{M}_{L}
on the left and a BC corresponding to a (not necessarily simple) object
m_{R}\in\mathcal{M}_{R}
on the right. The construction involves choosing (not necessarily simple) objects

\rho_{L}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{L},\qquad\rho_{R}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{R}\,,

(4.6)

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{x}
for
x\in\{L,R\}
is the module category for
\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}_{x}}
whose dual category is
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.

(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\mathcal{M}_{x}})^{*}_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{x}}=\mathcal{C}\,.

(4.7)

Then the Hilbert space of the model is generated by configurations of the form

(4.8)

where
m_{L}
and
m_{R}
“stretch to infinity” in the above figure.
\mu_{L}
and
\mu_{R}
are 2-morphisms in the 2-category
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S})
formed by module categories of
\mathcal{S}
, which physically is the 2-category formed by topological BCs of the 3d SymTFT
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathcal{S})
.

To describe the Hamiltonian, we need to choose boundary pieces
h_{L}
and
h_{R}
of the Hamiltonian along with the bulk piece
h
discussed above. These are

\displaystyle h_{L}

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \rho_{L}\otimes\rho\to\rho_{L}\otimes\rho h_{R}

\displaystyle:\penalty\ \rho\otimes\rho_{R}\to\rho\otimes\rho_{R}\,,

(4.9)

which should be viewed as 2-morphisms in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{S})
.

The
\mathcal{S}
symmetry acts from below, and modifies
(m_{L},m_{R})
, thus changing this model with BCs to another model with BCs, where all the input data remains the same except for the modification of
m_{L}
and
m_{R}
.
